Market analysis

OIL FUTURES: Diesel rallies as Ukraine resumes attacks on Russian refineries
ULSD futures surge 10.9 cents Nov. 3
Russian refining capacity estimated at 47% offline
US refiners operate at maximum utilization
NYMEX front-month ULSD futures rallied 10.9 cents to settle at $4.5306/gal on Nov. 3 following the resumption of Ukrainian drone attacks on Russian refineries.
The rally pushed the NYMEX December ULSD crack spread against WTI $4.95 higher to settle at $94.20/b.
The December ULSD crack had fallen from roughly $108/b in mid-October to $89.84/b on Oct. 29, as Ukraine did not attack any Russian refineries between Oct. 16 and Oct. 29.
However, Ukraine resumed attacks on Oct. 30, damaging four refineries since then, most recently against Russia's Yanos refinery.
"The massive toll taken on Russian refining by Ukrainian drone strikes over the past few days is BULLISH for global diesel cracks," said S&P Global Energy CERA analyst James Bambino. "We estimate nearly 47% of Russia's more than 7 million b/d of crude and condensate refining is now offline, with nearly 17%-19% taken offline in the last few days alone."
NYMEX front-month RBOB futures climbed 3.54 cents to settle at $2.9355/gal, putting the December crack spread at $27.20/b, up $1.86 on the day.
Crude futures were initially lifted by news of US strikes on Iran's Qeshm Island, but eventually settled lower. NYMEX frontmonth crude fell 37 cents to settle at $96.09/b, while ICE frontmonth Brent fell $1.17 to settle at $103.60/b.
(continued on page 19)

International Crude
India's crude imports hit record high in Oct as refiners stock up for festivals, product exports
Imports hit 5.54 million b/d, surpass March 2025 levels
Freight costs pressure Russian crude economics
Refiners maximize runs as crude supplies recover
Crude imports by Indian refiners rose to an all-time high of 5.54 million b/d in October, as refiners capitalized on improved availability to build supplies ahead of festival demand, while processing higher volumes to produce additional middle distillates for international markets facing supply tightness, industry sources and analysts said Nov. 3.
Domestic-bound crude imports in October were up 9% from both September and the same month a year earlier, according to data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea. The monthly average surpassed the previous high of 5.50 million b/d imported in March 2025.
"Russian imports have come off the peaks we saw in June and July, but they're still close to 2 million b/d in October. At the same time, we are seeing more barrels coming out of the Middle East, particularly from Saudi Arabia and Iraq, and Indian refiners are taking advantage of that availability ahead of stronger seasonal demand," said Premasish Das, executive director for oil analytics at S&P Global Energy CERA.
Russian crude shipments to India rose sharply in the first half of 2026, increasing from 1.17 million barrels/day in January to a peak of 2.43 million b/d in June, according to CAS. Flows eased to 2.05 million b/d in July, 1.98 million b/d in August, and 1.64 million b/d in September, but picked up to nearly 2.1 million b/d in October.
"An Oct. 16 US waiver reduced the immediate risk of additional measures affecting buyers of Russian crude. However, the waiver has no specified expiry and remains revocable, while existing Russia-related sanctions continue to leave importers exposed to renewed policy pressure," said Saransh Katiyar, liquid bulk analyst at CAS.
US President Donald Trump signed an executive order Oct. 16, waiving measures included in the Lindsey O. Graham Sanctioning Russia and Iran Act for the largest buyers of Russian oil, citing "national interests," days before they were due to take effect.

November 3, 2026

The legislation targeted buyers of Russian hydrocarbons, allowing the president to impose tariffs of up to 100% on goods imported from the five largest buyers of Russian crude oil or natural gas by total volume over the year before enactment, among other measures.
Countries such as India can continue to import Russian crude without immediately facing additional US penalties or tariffs. However, the waiver does not permanently revoke the law, and it can be withdrawn. As a result, buyers may choose to diversify their oil supplies and remain cautious about future sanctions risks, refining sources said.
Freight economics CERA's Das added that the economics of Russian barrels
were changing as freight costs remain high. Uncertainty around US concessions on Russian oil, the impact of drone attacks on Russian refining, and competition from Chinese buyers also mean Indian refiners will continue to look for opportunities across different sources of supply, depending on availability and relative attractiveness.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, last assessed Dirty Black Sea to West Coast India freight at a lumpsum of $28 million on Oct. 30, nearly double the year-to-date average of $14.5 million.
Analysts said the increase in October crude imports for Indian refiners was also supported by higher arrivals from Angola and the US.
"Favorable transatlantic arbitrage economics encouraged additional US crude flows into India, while stronger liftings of Angolan heavy-sweet and medium-sour grades also contributed to the rise," Katiyar said.
US crude flows in 2026 averaged 125,000 b/d over JanuaryOctober, with inflows reaching 150,000 b/d in October, up 16% from September levels, CAS data showed.
In addition, Middle Eastern producers were making a clear effort to regain market share, giving Indian refiners a wider range of supply options and contributing to the increase in overall imports, Das said, adding that India's crude imports were likely to remain elevated, supported by strong refining margins and the start-up of new capacity.

Improving availability "Indian refiners are swiftly capitalizing on improved crude
availability to maximize utilization rates and secure more cargoes to meet both festive demand at home and export more volumes to deficit markets," said B. Anand, former CEO of Nayara Energy and founder of consultancy firm Meridian Partners.
Indian refineries processed 5.62 million b/d in September, the oil ministry's provisional estimates showed, reflecting 9.1% higher processing from a year earlier as imports by the world's thirdlargest buyer rose despite supply concerns due to the Middle East conflict.
The crude processed in September was 6.6% higher than the month's target of 5.27 million b/d, it said. The processed volume in September was 2.7% higher than 5.47 million b/d in August, the ministry said.
Analysts said India's increase in crude imports in October may partly reflect a buildup of delayed cargoes rather than a sustained change in buying patterns. The movement of crude through the Strait of Hormuz, which remained partially open, also affected the timing and routing of shipments, they added.
"While the headline number on India's crude imports looks impressive, it is still not business as usual and can be a factor of supplies being bunched and refiners looking at stocking up available barrels," said Rajat Kapoor, managing director for oil and gas at Synergy Consulting.
"Refiners have been buying any cargo they can find in order to run plants above nameplate capacity and keep the domestic market supplied ahead of the Diwali festival season," he said.
India's total seaborne crude discharges, including volumes transferred ship-to-ship and subsequently redistributed to other markets, rose 19% month over month to 6.4 million b/d in October, CAS data showed.
-- Sambit Mohanty
Kazakhstan resumes crude flows through Atyrau-Samara pipeline: ministry
Pumping resumes after technical inspection
Ukrainian drone strikes Samara hub Nov. 2
Pipeline shipped 7.573 mil mt in Jan-Sept
Kazakhstan has resumed crude flows through the AtyrauSamara pipeline following a one-day halt, the Kazakh energy ministry told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, Nov. 3.
"As of Nov. 3, 2026, oil transportation via the Atyrau-Samara pipeline is proceeding as normal. Pumping operations were resumed following a technical inspection," the ministry said in a statement.
The Samara production and dispatch hub in Russia came under a Ukrainian drone attack Nov. 2, after which oil transportation through the Atyrau-Samara pipeline was suspended, according to the ministry.
State pipeline operator KazTransOil, which operates the Kazakh section of the Atyrau-Samara line, did not respond to a

The Atyrau-Samara route has served as an alternative to Kazakhstan's main supply route -- the CPC pipeline -- which has suffered significant disruptions because of drone attacks and technical and weather-related incidents.
Russia's Transneft, which operates the Samara hub, did not respond to a Platts request for comment.

CRUDE MOC: Middle East crude differentials edge higher
Platts Dubai, Oman premiums firm 62 cents/b DOD
Five Dubai partials trade during MOC
Cash differentials for Middle East sour crude markers firmed from 3-month lows during the Singapore Platts Market on Close assessment process Nov. 3.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed January cash Dubai, Oman and Murban all at a premium of 92 cents/barrel to same-month Dubai futures at the market close, up 62 cents/b day over day.
During the MOC, five January Dubai partials of 25,000 barrels each traded. The sellers were Mercuria and TotalEnergies, while the buyers were Shell and ExxonMobil.

Platts West African Crude Daily Commentary
Medium-heavy crude values recover sharply
Freight rate softening lifts FOB differentials
Nigerian November cargoes mostly clear market
Medium-heavy crudes saw a marked recovery in value in recent weeks, according to traders in West Africa on Nov. 3, as more bearable freight economics for buyers eased pressure on free-on-board pricing.
While differentials remained subdued relative to historical averages, a WAF-based trader told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, that grades from Angola and beyond had recovered by several dollars per barrel since the start of December trade.
Value for Chad's Doba crude was indicated above the $20/ barrel discount to Dated Brent threshold by the WAF trader, and an offer in recent days near that figure resulted in a trade "straight away."
A second trader said that the benchmark freight rate softening, for both physical fixtures and related derivatives, would, "In the absence of anything else, give some space for FOB differentials to increase."

"A feature of this market is that buyers don't want to buy in fear of prices going down, and sellers don't want to sell in fear of prices going up. People can hold wildly different opinions in this market," the second WAF trader said.
The Nigerian complex was heard to have mostly cleared what remained of its unsold November cargoes, with several cargo slippages into December aiding a net tightening in the market.
December trading was not heard to have kicked into gear after loading schedules were circulated over the past fortnight. Asian tenders were locking cargoes away from the spot market, according to a third trader.
Allocations of Nigerian crude to the Dangote refinery were expected to total about 6 million barrels in December, according to a fourth trader, who named Bonny Light, Qua Iboe, Amenam and Escravos as grades set for the domestic plant.

Americas Crude
Canadian heavy crude sees record weakness amid oversupply, extreme freight
USGC exports halt, Cushing stocks climb
Freight surge drives WCS to record discount
Heavy Canadian crude differentials widened to record lows as freight costs continued to prohibit exports, contributing to oversupply on the US Gulf Coast.
Canadian crude unable to ship from the USGC has also contributed to rising inventory in Cushing, which has increased since the week ended Sept. 10, according to US Energy Information Administration data. For the week ended Oct. 22, Cushing inventories reached 27 million barrels, up from 21.5 million barrels on Sept. 10 and 18.6 million barrels on July 23.
"I think USGC weakness is making its way up," one Canadian crude trader said.
US re-exports of Canadian crude from the USGC have fallen to zero barrels/day since August, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data.
Freight rates rose sharply from Aug. 14 through Oct. 9 amid stronger global freight markets and the war with Iran. VLCC rates from the USGC to China increased by $232.04/metric ton to $263.52/mt, while rates to the UK rose by $120.37/mt to $135.18/ mt. By Nov. 2, those rates had fallen to $237.04/mt and $114.81/mt, respectively, but remained elevated.
Western Canadian Select differentials to the NYMEX WTI CMA have widened since Sept. 10 across Hardisty, Cushing and Nederland. WCS Cushing reached a record discount of $24.75/b to the NYMEX WTI CMA on Oct. 27, while WCS ex-Nederland reached a record $23.40/b discount on Oct. 28.
WCS at Nederland, Texas, reached another all-time low

Gulf Coast faces heavy crude oversupply Rising Venezuelan crude imports have added another
source of heavy, sour crude to the USGC. US imports reached 782,000 b/d during the week ended Sept. 11, the highest level since August 2017, according to the EIA. Monthly imports have exceeded 500,000 b/d since May 2026.
Merey 16 delivered to the USGC fell by $20.265/b from Oct. 8 to Oct. 21. Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the grade at a $21.885/b discount to the Latin American WTI strip on a DAP basis at the USGC on Nov. 2.
Despite higher Venezuelan imports, US imports of Canadian crude have remained near typical 2026 levels, consistently exceeding 3.8 million b/d, according to the EIA.
Ripple effects in Canada WCS Hardisty reached a four-year low of a $31.05/b discount
to the WTI CMA on Oct. 26, as weaker US demand weighed on Canadian crude. The differential remained above the grade's overall low of a $50.35/b discount, assessed on Oct. 10, 2018.
Before the Trans Mountain Expansion pipeline began operations, WCS Hardisty was highly sensitive to apportionment on US-bound pipelines. With sufficient westbound takeaway capacity now filling the TMX system, Hardisty differentials have increasingly followed US pricing.
Higher Vancouver freight rates have also pressured Pacific Canadian grades. Vancouver-China and Vancouver-US West Coast Aframax rates reached records on Oct. 15 at $200/mt and $72.20/ mt, respectively, before falling to $146.25/mt and $68.40/mt on Nov. 2.
Pacific Cold Lake and Pacific Dilbit consequently reached record-low differentials since Platts began assessing the grades in April 2024. On Nov. 2, they were assessed at discounts of $17.51/b and $18.91/b to the WTI CMA, respectively, down $1.33/b from their previous assessments.

VENEZUELA DATA: Crude output edges up to 1.230 million b/d in October: preliminary data
Orinoco Belt crude production dips to 687,000 b/d
Output from western states up 12,000 b/d at 371,000 b/d
Venezuela's crude oil output averaged 1.230 million barrels per day in October, up from 1.218 million b/d in September, according to preliminary data from the Ministry of Hydrocarbons seen by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, Nov. 3.
Production continues to be affected by prolonged power outages, especially in the west of the country, as well as delays in upgrading the infrastructure that supports oil operations, according to the report.
Western fields Overall output from fields in the western states of Zulia and
Trujillo averaged 371,000 b/d in October, up from 359,000 b/d reported for September.
Of that, fields operated by state-owned PDVSA in the west produced 71,000 b/d, more or less steady on the month, while production from joint ventures and under Hydrocarbon Productive Participation Contracts (CPPHs) rose to 300,000 b/d in October from 288,000 b/d in September.
The Petroboscan JV between PDVSA (60%) and Chevron (40%) produced 115,925 b/d, edging up from 115,307 b/d in September.
Petrozamora, a JV between PDVSA and North American Blue Energy Partners (NABEP), produced 106,583 b/d in October, up from the 98,369 b/d recorded in September.
Production at seven other fields in Zulia state awarded to NABEP -- Lagunillas Lago, Intercampo Norte, Tia Juana Lago, Lagomar, Rosa Medio, Lago Medio, and Lago 5 -- totaled 51,900 b/d in October, up from the September average of 51,493 b/d.
NABEP attributed the increase in production to the addition of equipment for repairing oil wells.
"NABEP has nine drilling rigs, which bore new wells, in Venezuela. Three are active in the Junín 10 block of the Orinoco Belt, three more will start there soon, and three are being refurbished or moved into position. Four more are being shipped from Houston, Brazil and Colombia," the company said in a statement Oct. 31.
At the end of August, the US reached a deal with NABEP to expand oil production in Venezuela. As part of the deal, NABEP will have operating control of 17 fields in Venezuela.
Production in other oil fields in Zulia state was as follows: Petroregional del Lago (PDVSA 60%, Maurel & Prom 40%), 22,190 b/d; Petroquiriquire Occidente (PDVSA 60%, Spanish company Repsol 40%), 35,001 b/d; and Petrocabimas (Pacific Coast Energy Company) 10,414 b/d.
In fields located in the states of Barinas and Apure, operated by the Colombian company Colven under a productive production contract, production averaged 12,000 b/d in October.

Orinoco Belt Production in the Orinoco Belt, Venezuela's main oil-producing
area, fell to 687,000 b/d in October from 690,000 b/d reported in September, according to the preliminary data.
The Orinoco Belt is divided in four main blocks: Carabobo, Ayacucho, Junín and Boyacá. Production from the Boyacá block has remained shut in this year.
Additional details were not available.
East fields and offshore production Fields located in Monagas state produced 140,000 b/d in
October, steady on the month. Monagas state produces the Mesa and Santa Barbara light
crudes that PDVSA uses in its refining system and also to dilute extra-heavy crudes from the Orinoco Belt.
Offshore production was 15,400 b/d and while PDVSA Gas produced 4,600 b/d in October.
For September, Venezuela reported production of 1.206 million b/d to OPEC, while secondary sources estimated production at 1.150 million b/d, according to the group's Monthly Oil Market Report.
The Ministry of Hydrocarbons report contains preliminary information that can change after consolidation. The preliminary data does not include condensate production, which is estimated at 57,000 b/d for October, according to the report.
-- Mery Mogollon
ALASKA DATA: North Slope oil output rises 25,000 b/d in October
Pikka lifts North Slope production
October output up 67,648 b/d on year
Pikka near first-phase goal of 80,000 b/d
Alaska North Slope production gained 25,037 b/d on average in October over September as producers expanded output in new projects, according to the Alaska Department of Revenue daily and monthly production reports. The October average production was also up 67,648 b/d from October, 2025, before Pikka, a new oil field, went into production.
Pikka is being developed by partners Australia-based Santos, Ltd. and Repsol, based in Madrid, with Santos as operator. Total North Slope production averaged 524,740 b/d, up from 499,703 b/d in September and 457,082 b/d in October 2025. Pikka's production was near 80,000 b/d, reaching a third-quarter 2026 goal for Santos and Repsol.
Production from Pikka is measured through meters on the Kuparuk field pipeline, which carries Pikka's crude oil shipped to Pump Station One at the Trans Alaska Pipeline System at Prudhoe Bay. The Kuparuk pipeline throughput increased to an average of 164,680 b/d in October, up from 158,261 b/d in September. The bulk of the increase was due to Pikka, although there are also smaller new projects in the Kuparuk River field, which is owned and operated by ConocoPhillips.

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

With the first-phase production goal of 80,000 b/d achieved, Santos and Repsol are now planning a phase two expansion. That would add another 80,000 b/d with additional facilities and production wells brought online, Santos has said. Other North Slope fields produced as expected in October, with a gradual seasonal uptick as cooler northern Alaska weather set in. The Prudhoe Bay field, operated by Hilcorp Energy and the largest on the North Slope, averaged 300,343 b/d in October, up from 285,914 in September.
The state data showed the Kuparuk River field, operated and owned by ConocoPhillips, increasing to 164,680 in October, up from 158,261 b/d in September. Part of this is the new Pikka field output, with its oil shipped through the Kuparuk pipeline and metered. Kuparuk is adjacent to and west of the Prudhoe Bay field. The Alpine field, owned and operated by ConocoPhillips, produced 41,616 b/d in October, up from 38,703 b/d in September. Alpine is west of the Kuparuk River field and north of Pikka.
In other North Slope developments, ConocoPhillips is continuing construction at its new Willow field northwest of the Alpine and Pikka fields and in the federally-owned National Petroleum Reserve-Alaska. Willow is expected to begin producing in early 2029 with peak production of 180,000 b/d, ConocoPhillips has said.
On a related matter, the Department of Revenue also tracks crude oil inventories in storage at the Valdez Marine Terminal at the southern terminus of the Trans Alaska Pipeline System. The October storage data reflects the increased output from Pikka as well as shipments from Valdez to refineries. Crude in storage at Valdez reached 4 million barrels on Oct. 27, then dropped to 2.8 million barrels on Oct. 30 as tankers were loaded and departed. On Oct. 1, the crude oil in inventory was 1.9 million barrels. Crude oil stored in Valdez is in tanks that are part of the Valdez Marine Terminal, owned and operated by Alyeska Pipeline Service Co.
-- Tim Bradner

front-month ICE Brent crude oil futures fell 61 cents/b day over day, hitting a more than 6-month low of $9.92/b. The crack was last lower on April 30 at $7.91/b, Platts data showed.

Diesel
Weekly Russian diesel exports rise, while Ukraine resumes refinery attacks
Weekly diesel shipments hit 416,000 mt
US can receive record Russian diesel volumes
Refinery strikes pause for two weeks, then resume
Russian diesel and gasoil exports rose in the week ended Oct. 29, before Ukraine resumed its attacks on Russian refineries, a development that may reverse the increase in exports in the coming weeks.
Ukraine did not attack Russian refineries from Oct. 16-29, which marked the longest strike-free period for Russia's refining system in more than seven months.
Ukraine resumed its attacks on the refineries on Oct. 30, with damage reported at four refineries since then. The renewed attacks on Russian refineries "could lead to a reduction in supply volumes," according to Russia's Deputy Prime Minister Alexander Novak.
Meanwhile, Russia exported 416,000 metric tons of diesel and gasoil in the week ended Oct. 29, up from 306,000 mt the previous week. These exports marked the highest weekly level since the week started June 5, when Russia exported around 559,000 mt, according to preliminary S&P Global Commodities at Sea data.

Among Russian ports, 216,000 mt was loaded in Primorsk in the Baltic Sea, up from 204,000 mt a week earlier. Ust-Luga loaded 37,000 mt, lower than the 67,000 mt seen in the week before, and 60,000 mt loaded at Novorossiysk in the Black Sea, up from 35,000 mt the previous week. The remainder was loaded mainly in the Far East.
Of these loadings, 136,000 mt of product is expected to go to the US, up from the 76,000 mt loaded the previous week. These loadings marked the highest weekly shipments of diesel and gasoil to the US on record based on data going back to 2016.
Even so, none of these deliveries have yet reached the US shores, CAS data indicates, and they could still be diverted somewhere else.
Meanwhile, around 48,000 mt was loaded for Malaysia and 95,000 mt for Egypt, with the remainder unknown, according to preliminary CAS data.

Around 279,000 mt of Russian diesel/gasoil was preliminarily loaded for the US in October. The last time Russian diesel and gasoil was loaded for the US was in January 2022.
Additionally, around 175,000 mt was loaded for Turkey in October, up from 60,000 mt in September. Loadings for Egypt reached 130,000 mt, also higher than the 70,000 mt in September, according to CAS data.
Total loadings of Russian diesel and gasoil during October reached 1.1 million mt, whereas the total in September was roughly 453,000 mt. In October 2025, total Russian diesel and gasoil loadings reached around 2.7 million mt.
Ukraine will continue attacking Russian refineries until Moscow stops striking its energy infrastructure or until the US supplies new Patriot air defense missiles to Ukraine for one month, President Volodymyr Zelenskyy said on Nov. 1.
Ukraine has faced pressure from the US administration in the past weeks to stop attacking Russian refineries, with President Donald Trump repeatedly blaming Ukraine for high diesel prices.

according to the ICE Commitments of Traders report released Oct. 30, as the market focused on potential talks to end the Iran war and the US midterm elections.
Long positions classified under managed money fell by 6,563 contracts on the week to 69,059 contracts, from 75,622 contracts on Oct. 20. Gross long positions fell more sharply, dropping 9.3%, to 88,523 contracts. Managed money spreading positions rose 7,386 contracts, or 9.7%, to 83,453 contracts, suggesting greater spread trading positioning as traders reduced outright bullish exposure.

Marine Fuel
Australia completes first green ammonia bunkering of ammonia-powered bulker
Port Hedland refuels ammonia-powered ship
300-meter Mineral Europa loads iron ore to China
Two-hour transfer completes safety trial
Australia has completed its first green ammonia bunkering of an ammonia-powered dry bulk carrier at the Port of Port Hedland, marking another step toward the use of ammonia as a lower-emission marine fuel, according to a media statement by Fortescue and Pilbara Ports on Nov. 3
The 300-meter Mineral Europa, described as the world's first ammonia-powered dry bulk vessel, received certified green ammonia from a bunker vessel before entering the port to load iron ore.
The Mineral Europa is on charter to Fortescue and will transport Fortescue iron ore to customers in China.
The bunkering trial involved Pilbara Ports, Fortescue and CMB. TECH, alongside regulators and operational teams, to safely carry out the transfer in a live port environment.
The operation was completed within two hours and confirmed successful at 8:42 am Australian Western Standard Time on Nov. 2.
The trial follows a ship-to-ship ammonia transfer completed at the Port of Dampier in 2024 and supports Pilbara Ports' Clean Fuel Bunkering Hub initiative, which is exploring the establishment of a clean-fuel bunkering hub in the Pilbara.
Safety planning for the Port Hedland operation included hazard and risk assessments, ship-to-ship compatibility studies, ammonia dispersion modeling, emergency response planning, vessel vetting and pilotage and towage arrangements.
The transfer was conducted under operating limits covering weather and sea conditions, with additional marine support, vessel traffic management measures and vessel readiness requirements.
Pilbara Ports said insights from the trial would be reviewed to

Green ammonia is produced using renewable energy and contains no carbon, meaning it does not produce carbon dioxide emissions when used as a marine fuel, according to the statement.
Fortescue has also been testing ammonia as a marine fuel through its Green Pioneer vessel. In addition to the Mineral Europa, Fortescue charters another nine CMB.TECH vessels that can be converted to operate on ammonia in the future.

Resid
Singapore Nov ex-wharf HSFO premiums strengthen on expectations of resupply cuts
High freight costs block arbitrage imports
Stronger HSFO cargo market lifts ex-wharf valuations
Recovery of Russian flows to ease tightness
Ex-wharf term premiums for November 380 CST high sulfur fuel oil barrels in Singapore rose to the high-$60s/mt to $70/ mt against the benchmark FOB Singapore 380 CST HSFO cargo values, compared with premiums in the high-$50s/mt in October, amid expectations of tightened resupply as high freight costs curb imports, four Singapore-based traders told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy Nov. 3.
November and even December HSFO arrivals in Singapore are anticipated to decline, as arbitrage opportunities shut, following the steep climb in freight rates since late September, two Asiabased traders said.
Despite multiple export tenders in October for HSFO cargoes loading from inside the Persian Gulf, flows toward Singapore and Asia were challenged by steep additional war risk premiums, priced into freight and cargo insurance, due to the escalation in ship attacks around the Strait of Hormuz during the month, the traders noted.
Meanwhile, firm levels of downstream demand for HSFO bunker fuel could compound inventory draws, market participants said.
Singapore's commercial stockpiles of heavy distillates fell to a two-week low of 18.68 million barrels in the week ended Oct. 28, according to Enterprise Singapore data released late Oct. 29.
Nevertheless, earlier cargo fixtures, prior to the freight hike, and a mild recovery of Russian inflows to Singapore, could alleviate near term supply constraints, trade sources told Platts.
Singapore imported 99,989 mt of fuel oil from Russia in the week to Oct. 28, the first in 17 weeks, according to Enterprise Singapore data.
Middle Eastern arrivals have also risen for the last three consecutive weeks, with the week to Oct. 28 seeing a 26% increase in such volumes from the preceding week, the data showed.

"October freight was cheaper, and cargoes can come out of the Strait of Hormuz more easily," a trader said.
Platts assessed the Singapore 380 CST HSFO cash differential to the Mean of Platts Singapore 380 CST HSFO strip assessment at an average premium of $47.25/mt in October, up from the September average premium of $39.31/mt, on the back of increased refining margins and strong demand for high sulfur straight-run fuel oil as refinery feedstocks.
The M1/M2 intermonth spread for the 380 CST HSFO grade also averaged a wider backwardation of $51.05/mt in October, compared with $31.27/mt in September, Platts data showed.
Like the Singapore low sulfur fuel oil bunker market, delivered premiums for the HSFO grade have lately been at elevated levels, driven by early November cargo tightness and steady demand, keeping downstream margins fairly healthy.
The spread between the Singapore-delivered 380 CST HSFO and the equivalent ex-wharf grade, also known as the barge spread, widened to $17.23/mt on average in October, from the $6.82/mt September average, Platts data showed.
Platts also assessed the Singapore-delivered spot 380 CST HSFO bunker premiums over the FOB Singapore 380 CST HSFO cargo assessments at an average of $83.26/mt over October, surging 69.3% from September, when the premiums averaged $49.19/mt.
-- Iris Leung
Platts London MOC October fuel oil traded volumes fall 17% on month
HSFO barge volumes drop to 286,000 mt
VLSFO trading plunges to 126,000 mt total
The volume of fuel oil traded during the London Platts Market on Close assessment process in October declined 17% month over month to 412,000 metric tons, according to data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
Volumes traded in the high sulfur fuel oil barge market fell marginally to 286,000 mt in October from 298,000 mt in September, Platts data showed.
The largest buyers were BP and Mercuria, while Vitol and Aramco were the largest sellers, the data showed.
In the Mediterranean HSFO CIF Mediterranean cargo market in October, there were no trades observed during the Platts MOC process for a third consecutive month.
The volume traded in the very low sulfur fuel oil barge market fell to 126,000 mt in October, down from 200,000 mt in September, the data showed.
The largest buyer was TotalEnergies, while Glencore and ExxonMobil were the largest sellers.
No very low sulfur fuel oil cargoes were traded during the MOC process in October.
For low sulfur fuel oil, no trades were recorded in either the Northwest European Rotterdam barge or CIF Mediterranean cargo markets in October.

Singapore MOC fuel oil swaps trades fall 27% MOM in Oct to 2-month low
HSFO swaps trade volume drops 26.8% MOM in Oct
LSFO swaps trades shrink 27% to four-month low
The volume of Singapore fuel oil derivatives traded during the Platts Market on Close assessment process in October fell 26.8% month over month to 19.84 million barrels as the volumes of both high and low sulfur fuel oil deals shrank from September, Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, data showed.
The total fuel oil swaps trades, which surged to a four-year high in September, have now recorded their lowest monthly volume since August 2022, when 16.35 million barrels of fuel oil swaps were traded. Overall, October trades were 92.5% higher than the 10.31 million barrels traded in the corresponding month in 2025, the data showed.
The volume of Singapore high-sulfur fuel oil swaps trades decreased 26.8% month over month to 17.18 million barrels in October, marking the lowest monthly volume for the residual grade since August, when 11.81 million barrels of HSFO were traded, according to Platts data.
Out of the total HSFO swaps traded in October, 12.99 million barrels were benchmark 380 CST HSFO trades, compared with 20.16 million barrels in September. The volume of 180 CST HSFO trades increased about 7% month over month to 1.43 million barrels, the data showed.
The monthly decline in traded volumes comes amid firmer time spreads for the Singapore HSFO swaps. The M1-M2 intermonth spread for the 380 CST HSFO grade averaged at a steeper backwardation of $51.05/metric ton in October, compared with an average of $31.27/mt in the preceding month, Platts data showed.
The Asian HSFO market continues to gain strength from a persistent supply crunch, as attacks on vessels in the Strait of Hormuz keep flaring supply concerns from the Middle East. However, some market sources said easing utility demand after the summer months could free up supplies and improve market balance in the near term.
Strait of Hormuz shipping traffic rose to its highest monthly level since July in October, supported by stronger dry-bulk and LNG movements despite a decline in VLCC traffic and mounting regional threats, S&P Global Commodities at Sea data showed Nov. 2.
Platts assessed the Singapore 380 CST HSFO cargo's cash differential to the MOPS 380 CST HSFO assessment at a premium of $49.92/mt at the Asian close Nov. 2, drifting lower from a more than six-week high of $51.22/mt on Oct. 30.
Meanwhile, Singapore marine fuel 0.5%S swaps trade volume

decreased 27% month over month in October to 2.67 million barrels, the lowest monthly volume in four months, since 2.1 million barrels traded in June, Platts data showed. The October trade volume for the IMO 2020-compliant grade, however, was 31.4% higher year over year, the data showed.
The M1-M2 intermonth spread for FOB Singapore 0.5%S marine fuel swaps has averaged at a backwardation of $33.33/mt in October, compared with an average of $40.64/mt in September, Platts data showed.
The Asian LSFO market remains relatively buoyant amid tightness in finished-grade supplies, as arbitrage arrivals from the western markets are expected to decline in November.
Singapore, the world's largest bunkering hub, is now expected to receive about 1.3 million-1.5 million metric tons of LSFO from the West in November, down from about 1.6 million-1.7 million mt in October.
Platts assessed the Singapore marine fuel 0.5%S cargo's differential to the Mean of Platts Singapore marine fuel 0.5%S assessment at a premium of $31.79/mt at the Asian close Nov. 2, inching down from $32.67/mt on Oct. 30, weighed by competitive offers for H2 November-loading cargoes.
In the physical market, the total volume of FOB Singapore fuel oil cargoes traded during the Platts Market on Close assessment process in October fell 77.5% month over month to 287,000 metric tons (about 58,789 b/d), weighed down by sizable declines in both high- and low sulfur fuel oil deals.
The physical trades for high sulfur fuel oil during the Platts Market on Close assessment process in October, which had more than tripled month over month to a total of 1.12 million mt in September, have dropped 83.3% to 187,000 in October, recording the smallest monthly volume of HSFO trades since January, when 180,000 mt HSFO was traded, Platts data showed.
The physical trades for FOB Singapore 0.5%S marine fuel stood at 100,000 mt in October, down 37.5% from 160,000 mt in September, Platts data showed. There were no trades for the IMO 2020-compliant marine fuel in October 2025, Platts data showed.

European propane-naphtha spread narrows to 8-month high
High gas costs tighten propane availability
Naphtha gains appeal as feedstock alternative
The Northwest European propane-naphtha spread narrowed to an eight-month high Nov. 2, driven by constrained propane supply due to high natural gas prices.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the European front-month propane-naphtha spread - the difference between the propane and the naphtha CIF NWE cargo swap prices - at minus $73.50/metric ton on Nov. 2, the highest since March 3, when it stood at minus $69/mt.

When the propane-naphtha spread rises, it increases naphtha's competitiveness as a petrochemical feedstock, which players say has stimulated greater buying interest in naphtha from the steam cracking sector.
"Naphtha in Europe is fairly supported because petrochemical demand is very strong," a trading source said.
On the physical front, Platts assessed the propane CIF Northwest Europe large cargoes at $847.25/mt Nov. 2, compared with $736/mt Oct. 1. Naphtha CIF NWE cargoes rose to $924.50/ mt from $864.75/mt over the same period.
The moves narrowed the physical propane-naphtha spread to minus $77.25/mt from minus $128.75/mt, a contraction of $51.50/ mt. Propane gained $111.25/mt between the two assessments, outpacing naphtha's $59.75/mt increase.
The narrowing of the spread has been driven by reduced propane availability, increasingly constrained by subdued regional production and competing fuel requirements.
"I think propane is stronger, [even] for a seasonal level," the trading source said.
Elevated natural gas costs encouraged refineries to retain LPG for internal fuel use, limiting volumes available to external buyers, while the start of the heating season added another source of demand.
"Some refineries are running propane because TTF is very expensive to run for making power," the trading source said, "That leaves some extra small pockets of demand for naphtha into the petrochemical sector."
Platts assessed the Dutch TTF day-ahead gas price at Eur94.38/megawatt-hour on Nov. 2, up significantly from Oct. 2, when it stood at Eur75/MWh. The level was also substantially above Nov. 3, 2025, which was assessed at Eur31.135/MWh.
Propane loadings from Norway declined by 20,500 mt year over year to 90,100 mt in October 2026, from 110,600 mt in the same month a year earlier, S&P Global Commodities at Sea data showed.

Gas Liquids
Energy Transfer boosts NGL output as Permian activity rises
NGL output jumps 10.4% on Permian activity
Ethane export capacity expands to 300,000 b/d
Tenth fractionator adds 200,000 b/d by 2029
US midstream operator Energy Transfer LP reported higher NGL production, transportation and fractionation volumes in the third quarter of 2026, as increased Permian processing activity boosted liquids output, according to the company's Nov. 3 earnings press release.
NGL production increased to 1.269 million barrels/day during the quarter, up 10.4% from 1.149 million b/d a year earlier. Energy Transfer attributed the increase primarily to higher utilization of

new and existing processing plants in the Permian Basin in the Southwestern United States.
Gathered natural gas volumes also increased 6.3% year over year to 22.941 cubic feet/day, with gathering and processing volumes rising across most regions, driven by new and upgraded Permian plants and higher dry-gas gathering in the Northeast and Ark-La-Tex regions.
The increase in processing activity is relevant to the US ethylene market, as additional NGL recovery can increase the availability of components such as ethane for fractionation and for subsequent use as steam cracker feedstock.
Energy Transfer management said during its Q3 earnings call that Permian midstream volumes rose about 8% year over year, supported by new processing plants and improved utilization. The company expects volumes at the recently commissioned Mustang Draw II processing plant to ramp up through the first half of 2027.
Energy Transfer said higher Permian volumes and increased NGL exports contributed to higher transportation, fractionation and terminal throughput. NGL exports were up 9% year over year.
The company is also expanding infrastructure specifically dedicated to ethane exports. Energy Transfer is actively involved in significant projects in Nederland, Texas, including the development of an ethane cracker facility and expansions of its export infrastructure.
Energy Transfer said it had increased the scope of its planned Nederland ethane export expansion to about 300,000 b/d from 240,000 b/d, citing additional customer demand. The expansion is expected to enter service in stages beginning in 2028, with two additional NGL docks expected by mid-2029.
At Marcus Hook on the East Coast, an expansion scheduled for the second half of 2027 is expected to increase export capacity to 100,000 b/d. Energy Transfer said existing customers have contracted for around 75% of that capacity, with agreements extending into the late 2030s and early 2040s.
Growing NGL demand in India, South Korea, China and Europe is supporting further expansion at its Nederland and Marcus Hook export terminals. During the call, it was also noted that there was an "underlying pull globally" for US supplies and that demand was expected to continue supporting its assets at higher utilization rates.
The company's domestic processing and fractionation footprint is expanding alongside its export capacity. The 275 million cubic feet/d Mustang Draw II plant in the Midland Basin entered service in October, while the planned 200 MMcf/d Bear II cryogenic plant in the Delaware Basin is expected to be online in Q3 2027.
Further downstream, Energy Transfer reached a final investment decision on its tenth fractionator in Mont Belvieu. The 200,000 b/d facility is expected to be online in the first half of 2029, increasing the company's total fractionation capacity at Mont Belvieu to approximately 1.6 million b/d. Additional Y-grade volumes and Permian growth supported the decision to move forward with the project. The additional fractionation capacity

The combination of rising Permian processing volumes and additional fractionation capacity points to expanding infrastructure for US NGL production, while the company's decision to increase planned ethane export capacity highlights growing competition between domestic and overseas demand for US ethane.
For the US ethylene market, growing NGL processing and fractionation capacity could support longer-term feedstock availability for steam crackers. At the same time, greater ethane export capacity could provide an additional outlet for US supplies, making international ethane demand increasingly relevant to domestic feedstock balances.

India Oct LPG imports from UAE on par with pre-Middle East war inflows: CAS
US becomes second-largest LPG supplier
Total imports rise 14.36% from September
India took delivery of 646,300 metric tons of LPG, comprising 268,500 mt propane and 377,800 mt butane, from the UAE in October, largely on par with the 647,300 mt of LPG it imported in February, just before the start of the US-Israeli war with Iran, data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea showed Nov. 3.
The figure compares with 950,000 mt of LPG (319,300 mt propane and 328,000 mt butane) imported from the UAE in October 2025, still the highest volume India has imported from the UAE since CAS first started compiling the data in October 2016.
India imported a total of 1.513 million mt of LPG in October, comprising 725,000 mt propane and 785,000 mt butane, up 14.36% from September, but down 24.46% from October 2025.
Although there have been many spot LPG cargoes offered by Middle Eastern producers such as Kuwait Petroleum Corp. and Qatar Energy via public tenders in recent months amid the Middle East war and the effective closure of the Strait of Hormuz as well as disruption in the Bab al-Mandab strait, there have been few reports of the UAE offering spot LPG cargoes for sale.
According to a freight report by analysts at S&P Global Energy CERA Oct. 29, the number of vessels moored at Indian terminals during the week of Oct. 19-25 remained above the five-year range, while the number of laden VLGCs bound for the Indian subcontinent stayed within its five-year seasonal range.
"This pattern indicates that buyers continued to diversify their supply sources ahead of the region's peak demand season," the analysts said in the report. "The US remained the region's primary alternative source of LPG."
Middle East LPG exports continued to account for a relatively small share of global trade flows, with shipment volumes remaining well below the five-year range, the CERA report said.
"Nevertheless, activity has shown signs of recovery, with

weekly shipments increasing by 29% week over week. This increase was supported by the expanded use of shuttle vessels and ship-to-ship (STS) hubs located outside the Strait of Hormuz," the CERA report said.
India imported 138,000 mt of LPG from Kuwait in October, up from 92,000 in September, while imports from Qatar stood at 46,000 mt, down from 96,000 mt over the same period, according to the CAS data. India also imported 47,000 mt from Iran in October, down from 136,000 mt in September, and 40,000 mt from Oman, up from 6,000 mt in September. It also imported 46,000 mt from Algeria in October, the CAS data showed.
The US was the second-largest contributor to India's LPG imports at 517,000 mt, comprising 303,000 mt propane and 210,000 mt butane, holding on to its position for the second straight month, CAS data showed. India imported 397,000 mt of LPG from the US in September and 41,000 mt in October 2025.
During the week ended Oct. 23, US LPG exports to India totaled about 99,000 mt, up from 49,000 mt the previous week, as India entered its peak seasonal LPG demand period, CAS data released Oct. 27 showed.
-- Norazlina Jumaat
European MTBE premium over butane nearly halves in October as LPG tightens
MTBE prices drop 6.1% amid weak demand
Butane surges 33% on tight supply issues
Blending economics worsen as spreads narrow
The premium of European MTBE over butane narrowed by almost half during October, as a sharp rise in Northwest European butane prices contrasted with softer MTBE values, bringing the two gasoline blending components closer together in outright terms.
The Platts MTBE FOB ARA premium over butane FOB ARA fell to $438/mt Oct. 30 from $848.25/mt Oct. 1, a contraction of $410.25/mt, according to S&P Global Energy.
Prompt MTBE prices declined $108.25/mt across the same period, driven largely by weakening gasoline blending demand.
Gasoline blending economics deteriorated throughout October as the front-month gasoline-naphtha spread narrowed significantly, deterring consumption of more expensive octane boosters in the blend pool.
Market participants also noted alternative blendstock reformate becoming more favorable. The Platts FOB AR marker fell $152.25/mt from Oct. 1 to Oct. 30.
"You can skip MTBE into E5 blending now," one source said, acknowledging a decline in MTBE's competitive edge for blenders.
While elevated values initially saw support from persistently tight supply, heightened seller interest toward the end of the month provided downward pressure.
Six trades were completed in the Platts Market on Close assessment process in October, with Integra Petrochemicals

Europe AG notably purchasing 6,000 mt in total. Butane drove most of the narrowing, rising $302/mt, or 33%,
over the period to close at $1,216/mt. MTBE declined $108.25/mt, or 6.1%, to $1,654/mt.
The divergence became particularly pronounced early in the month. Butane rose from $936/mt Oct. 7 to $1,063/mt Oct. 8, while MTBE increased more modestly, compressing its premium from $803.50/mt to $695.75/mt in a single session.
The spread narrowed further toward month-end, falling from $559.75/mt Oct. 26 to $438/mt Oct. 30 as butane strengthened and MTBE lost ground. The closing differential was the narrowest in October.
Butane's strength reflected persistent constraints on prompt availability alongside seasonal gasoline blending demand. The transition to winter specifications allowed greater incorporation of butane into the gasoline pool, supporting consumption even as broader gasoline economics weakened.
Refiners retained more butane for their own blending requirements, restricting the volumes available to merchant buyers. Limited regional supply consequently sustained competition for spot barrels, while rising butane costs reduced its attractiveness as a petrochemical feedstock.
Import replacement offered limited relief. Stronger US winter gasoline blending demand absorbed butane domestically and reduced export availability, while constrained Middle Eastern supply tightened the wider global balance.

Maritime traffic through the Bab al-Mandab Strait rose further Nov. 2 to close to the highest levels seen in recent weeks, while movements through the Strait of Hormuz fell below October levels amid continued ship attacks, according to preliminary S&P Global Commodities at Sea data.

Tanker fleet growth threatens freight boom, bullish secondhand markets: analysts
Fleet capacity surges 23% through 2029 Supply growth risks eroding record rates Market uncertainty amid geopolitical conflicts
A wave of tanker deliveries is set to test whether geopolitical disruption and longer trading routes can continue supporting charter rates and ship values, according to S&P Global Energy CERA's latest fleet outlook, after an extraordinary freight market rally pushed some secondhand ship values above newbuilding order prices.
Taking newbuild order books and scrapping into account, CERA analysts forecast global tanker capacity will rise from 703.72 million deadweight tons at the end of 2025 to 743.56 million dwt in 2026, 778.35 million dwt in 2027 and 830.61 million dwt in 2028, before reaching 862.98 million dwt in 2029. That would represent cumulative growth of 22.6% over four years.
"The tanker market is entering a transition from scarcity-led strength to a period in which demand must work much harder to absorb supply," according to the fleet outlook published on Oct. 28. "The critical issue is not whether supply will grow, it clearly will, but whether ton-mile demand, scrapping, sanctions-related inefficiencies and operational segmentation can absorb that growth without a material decline in utilization and freight rates."
Record rates Tanker rates across segments have risen to record levels
following the outbreak of the US-Iran war at the end of February, boosted by increased ship requirements as Asian importers seek barrels from farther afield and Middle Eastern producers rely on

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

15

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

November 3, 2026

ship-to-ship transfers to mitigate transit risks around the Strait of Hormuz.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the its Global Dirty Tanker Index for non-scrubber-fitted, non-eco tonnage at $840,921.58/day on Nov. 2, compared with $161,876.1/d on Feb. 27, increasing more than 400%. Meanwhile, the Platts Global Clean Tanker Index for non-scrubber-fitted, non-eco tonnage has risen to $143,454.09/d from $38,131.69/d over the same period, increasing more than 275%.
The inefficiencies "cannot be assumed to expand indefinitely," CERA analysts said, while adding that normalizing geopolitical trade patterns, easing sanctions or the return of restricted ships to mainstream employment could increase effective supply more rapidly than the headline fleet figures indicate.
Newbuild deliveries The order book comprised 1,494 tankers totaling 184.10 million
dwt at the end of September, equivalent to 18.3% of the fleet by ship count but 25.1% by carrying capacity, according to CERA data. Very Large Crude Carriers and Suezmaxes accounted for about 70.5% of ordered capacity.
VLCC capacity is forecast to increase 31.5% from 273.31 million dwt in 2025 to 359.28 million dwt in 2029, while Suezmax capacity is projected to expand 30.6% to 143.13 million dwt, the data shows. Product tanker deliveries are more front-loaded, with Aframax/Long Range 2 and Medium Range growth expected to moderate after 2026-2027.
"The more challenging period is 2028-29, when VLCC and Suezmax deliveries become exceptionally large," CERA analysts said.
The freight boom has transformed tanker asset markets. CERA estimated a five-year-old VLCC at $185 million, up 57.4% over nine months and around $40 million above a newbuilding order price of $145 million.
The analysts described the VLCC market as displaying "one of the clearest inverted asset-value structures in modern shipping history," as ships in operation can capture record earnings immediately.
Ship values XClusiv Shipbrokers assessed October newbuilding prices at
$131.5 million for a VLCC, $90.5 million for a Suezmax, $78 million for an Aframax and $52.5 million for an MR2, 4%, 5%, 8% and 8% higher year on year, respectively. Its secondhand estimates showed sharper increases, including a 10-year-old VLCC at $186 million, up 117% year on year, and a 15-year-old VLCC at $160.4 million, up 178%.
The market was characterized by "selective investment rather than uniform buying appetite," the Greek brokerage said in a Nov. 2 note. The brokerage recorded 371 tankers offered for sale during September-October against 146 transactions, with VLCCs and Suezmaxes representing 34% of completed deals but less than 8% of offers.
The figures suggested buying was "disproportionately concentrated in larger tankers," potentially reflecting stronger earnings expectations and confidence in crude transportation,

while the availability of older tonnage highlighted owners' willingness to "capitalize on current asset values," according to XClusiv Shipbrokers.
Veson Nautical similarly said five-year-old tanker values were 32% higher on average than in January, with VLCCs up 70% to $196.3 million, according to a Nov. 3 report. With values above pre-2008 highs and "low visibility on future chartering markets," Veson expected ordering to "calm."
The analytics firm's outlook indicates that tanker values will decline over the forecast period, particularly in the VLCC and Suezmax segments, as deliveries accumulate. But Veson expects full restoration of Middle East oil flows to remain unlikely before at least mid-2027, potentially prolonging the inefficiencies supporting near-term earnings and values.

Chinese refineries likely to increase Nov clean oil product exports with new quotas
Nov exports climb to 4 million mt with quotas
Gasoline shipments surge to 800,000 mt - 1 million mt range
Domestic supply concerns limit refiner plans
Chinese refineries are likely to increase their total clean oil product exports to about 4 million metric tons in November after being awarded new quotas, three Beijing-based sources close to the matter said Nov. 3.
Before the allocation at the end of October, the refiners were estimated to export about 3.13 million mt of gasoline, gasoil and jet fuel, compared with about 3.7 million mt in October.
The sources said gasoline exports were likely to rise from the original estimate of just over 300,000 mt to a range of 800,000 mt to 1 million mt, and jet fuel volumes would increase to about 2.4 million mt, while gasoil exports would remain unchanged from the initial export target of about 800,000 mt.
One of the sources said the increases were more likely to come from refineries processing domestic crude, as they would avoid high seaborne freight costs.
A South China-based Sinopec refiner said the plant had no plans to increase exports despite increased quota availability, citing tight domestic supply. Meanwhile, high crude prices and freight costs have pushed its gross profit into negative territory.
"Exports would help narrow the losses, but losses are still losses," he said.
"PetroChina will continue to conduct oil product exports in an orderly manner and on market-based principles, in line with government policy and taking into account domestic product supply and sales as well as export economics," PetroChina CFO Wang Hua said during the company's Q3 results call on Oct. 29.

Saudi Aramco working on East-West pipeline expansion, other export routes: CEO
Aramco advances engineering work on expansion
East-West pipe brought online days after attack
Saudi Arabia's East-West oil pipeline is undergoing preliminary engineering work for an expansion beyond its 7 million barrels/ day capacity, while more export routes are also being developed,

Saudi Arabian Oil Co. CEO Amin Nasser told analysts in a Nov. 3 earnings call.
"Preliminary engineering is underway for an expansion of the East-West pipeline," Nasser said, adding that, in line with energy minister Prince Abdulaziz bin Salman's Oct. 12 announcement of upcoming additional pipeline capacity, the company is working toward "major expansions [to Aramco's] capacity going much more than the 7 million barrels/day that we currently have."
Bin Salman had said at the WPC Energy Congress in Riyadh Oct. 12 that the kingdom planned to expand the East-West pipeline's capacity to up to 10 million b/d within two to three years, alongside plans to temporarily convert a gas pipeline to provide an estimated 1.2 million b/d of crude transport capacity within seven to eight weeks from then.
"We are also in the process of -- we're doing the engineering, actually -- other routes in addition to the current routes," Nasser said on Aramco's third-quarter earnings call Nov. 3.
Nasser declined to comment further on the pipeline expansion plans during the call and said projects are still in progress.
Pipeline attacks Saudi Arabian energy facilities faced multiple targeted aerial
attacks over the third quarter, with the East-West pipeline shut temporarily as a result, state media reported Sept. 11.
The pipeline returned to operations within five or six days after being attacked and was pumping 5.8 million b/d of crude Oct. 6, state media reported Bin Salman as saying that day.
Visible Saudi crude loadings from the Red Sea port of Yanbu resumed Sept. 23 after ceasing Sept. 12 following the attack on the pipeline, according to ship tracking data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea Nov. 3.
"Even with the attack on our East-West pipeline, we were able, within days, to bring it back and maximize exports through the Western region, as well as using our export terminal from the Gulf," Nasser said, highlighting Aramco's use of "inspection drones to assess damage in real time, so that repairs can commence as soon as it is safe."
Saudi Arabia also continued to ship crude while western loadings faced disruption through oil shuttling outside the Strait of Hormuz, performing ship-to-ship transfers "for customers who are not willing to come and load from our facilities," Nasser said.
Gross crude loadings from the kingdom averaged 6.1 million b/d in Q3, up from Q2's average of just over 5 million b/d, according to CAS data Nov. 3.
CAS could revise the estimates as more data becomes available. Saudi crude transported aboard ships with their automatic identification systems switched off may not always be captured in the estimates.
"And we expect, as we continue with exports utilizing all the routes that we have, which is either through the Red Sea or through the Mediterranean using the SUMED, or the Suez Canal and Port Said, or through the Gulf, to maximize our production going forward in the fourth quarter," Nasser said.
-- Lulwa Albuainain

Saudi Aramco CEO says world oil demand will rebound amid lower oil stocks
Nasser predicts 'stronger' oil demand return
Global inventories need 3 billion barrels: CEO
Diesel stocks hit 'alarmingly low' levels: CEO
Global oil demand "will come back stronger" as countries seek to rebuild depleted inventories and boost energy security, Saudi Arabian Oil Co. CEO Amin Nasser said Nov. 3, warning that refined product stocks were "alarmingly low."
The world has lost over 3 billion barrels of liquid supply since the start of the current US-Iran conflict, Nasser told analysts during a third-quarter earnings call. However, global crude oil demand today "has proven to be more robust than many anticipated earlier," Nasser said. "We don't see any structural decline in demand, but, rather, unmet demand due to supply constraints."
"Demand will come back stronger, not only to meet base demand growth, but also to build cushion for energy security, a priority that is now at the top of every country's agenda," Nasser said on the call, without giving a timeframe.
S&P Global Energy CERA expects global liquids demand to contract by 2.6 million barrels/day in 2026, before rebounding by 2.6 million b/d in 2027 and 1.5 million b/d in 2028.
Nasser said that Chinese imports of Saudi crude had "come back" in October.
Global seaborne crude oil loadings destined for China averaged 9.6 million b/d in October, up 3.1 million b/d from June, oil analysts at CERA estimated on Oct. 29.
China's crude imports from Saudi Arabia in September fell 62% year over year and 14% month over month to 2.7 million metric tons, or 644,341 b/d, data from the General Administration of Customs showed Oct. 20.
According to S&P Global Commodities at Sea, crude arrivals into China from Saudi Arabia in October are estimated to reach 1.1 million b/d.
Depleted stocks Beyond meeting additional demand, oil markets also face the
challenge of replenishing depleted inventories, Nasser said. In addition to the 2 billion barrels needed to replenish global
inventories over two years, countries will also need another 1 billion barrels to supply "the new storage facilities that countries and companies will start to build so that they don't end up with the current position that we are in today," Nasser said.
The disruption to Middle East Gulf energy flows has left global commercial onshore oil inventories at less than 6 billion barrels, the majority of which is "locked up," Nasser said at the WPC Energy Congress in Riyadh on Oct. 12.
Beyond oil, global product stocks are also "now alarmingly low, particularly for diesel," Nasser said during the Nov. 3 analyst call.
"With diesel demand expected to increase further in Q4, the market faces further pressure heading into winter," Nasser

said, adding that global liquids demand is projected to expand by around 2.4 million b/d in 2027 "as demand management measures ease."
Total OECD diesel/gasoil stocks drew counter-seasonally to 565 million barrels in July, down 5% from pre-conflict levels and their lowest since November 2023, the IEA said in it's latest monthly oil market report.
Global distillate markets remain severely constrained, with reduced Middle East and Russian exports, amounting to a shortfall of about 1.5 million b/d, according to CERA.
-- Lulwa Albuainain
Argentina on track to reach diesel, gasoline supply surplus on rising crude output
Refiners are increasing capacity
Crude output is at record high 973,700 b/d
Fuel imports fall 10.7% in first nine months of year
Argentina is on track to have a surplus in diesel and gasoline supplies, as refiners increase capacity to handle a rise in local oil output from the Vaca Muerta shale play, Energy Secretary María Tettamanti said Nov. 3.
"We already have a surplus of crude and we are going to soon have a surplus of diesel and gasoline," she said at the Fourth Natural Gas Day in Buenos Aires hosted by the Argentina Oil and Gas Institute, an industry group.
Tettamanti said refiners are investing to increase their capacity to substitute imports, which are largely of higher-quality products. This is helping to reduce imports in volume terms at a time when international prices are high because of the supply disruptions from the US-Iran war in the Middle East, she added.
"In the world today with the war where some countries have problems with fuel supply, we only have the issue of prices," Tettamanti said. "That's not a small issue, but we have the supply."
Argentina's fuel and lubricants imports fell 10.7% in the first nine months of this year in volume terms, but rose 23.3% in price, according to Indec, the national statistics agency. Over the same period, exports increased 29.5% in volume terms and 18.6% in the amount paid in US dollars, the data showed.
The decline in imports and rise in exports comes as oil production surges in Argentina, led by the Vaca Muerta shale play. Crude output shot up 15.5% to a record high of 973,700 b/d in September from the year-earlier month, with some 700,000 b/d coming from Vaca Muerta. This is making more crude available for export, given that the country consumes 500,000 b/d on average.
The shale play, in northern Patagonia, is expected to lead a rise in Argentina's total oil output to 1.7 million b/d in 2030, according to a forecast from the Argentinian Chamber of Hydrocarbons Exploration and Production, an industry group. By 2035, national oil production is expected to reach 2.2 million b/d, according to Aleph Energy, a consulting firm in Buenos Aires.
-- Charles Newbery

Refinery updates
REFINERY NEWS: CVR Energy sees lower Q4 rates ahead of Marathon purchase
Refinery: Coffeyville, Kansas, and Wynnewood, Oklahoma
Owner: CVR Energy
Overall capacity: 132,000 b/d and 74,500 b/d
Units affected: N/A
Duration: Q4 2026
CVR Energy sees its planned purchase of two Marathon Petroleum refineries as a way to add scale, cut its exposure to costly RINs, and widen its crude supply, CEO Dane Neumann said Nov. 3.
"From an operations perspective, these assets are similar to what we own and operate today, medium-sized inland refineries with advantaged crude supplies via Bakken and Canadian crude oil processing," he said during CVR's thirdquarter results call.
Earlier, CVR said it would pay $4.2 billion for Marathon's 105,000 barrel/day St. Paul Park, Minnesota, refinery and its 72,000 b/d Mandan, North Dakota, plant.
Combined with Coffeyville and Wynnewood, CVR's crude processing capacity would rise from about 207,000 b/d to about 384,000 b/d, catapulting them to the fourth largest refinery in PADD 2, up from their current ninth place. Neumann said the plants are well-maintained and have averaged over 90% utilization over the past five years.
Crude slates synergy Neumann said that out of about 175,000 b/d of additional
capacity from Marathon's plants, about 126,000 b/d is local Bakken crude. Canadian crude is just under 50,000 b/d, with about half of that being WCS or other heavy grades.
According to Q3 data, CVR's legacy plants ran 6,746 b/d of

For Q4 2026, CVR expects total throughput in its two legacy refineries to run between 210,000-225,000 b/d, slightly below the combined throughput of about 228,000 b/d in Q3. Q3 crude utilization was 104.9% and light product yield was 93%, with a distillate yield of 45.8%. The Group 3 2-1-1 crack averaged $68.50/barrel in Q3, compared with $25.94/b in Q3 2025.
Increased blending to mitigate RINs costs The cost of meeting the Renewable Fuel Standard (RFS)
has been rising, as RIN credit prices rose more than 125% from Q3 2025 to average more than $14/b in Q3 2026, according to Richard Roberts, CVR's CFO.
Net RIN expense in Q3, excluding the change in RFS liability, was $235 million, or $11.20/b, cutting the company's capture rate by about 16%, Roberts said.
With the addition of the two Marathon refinery acquisitions, which have a high blending capacity, CVR's internal RIN generation will be increased, lessening the need for CVR to rely on third-party RIN purchases.
Neumann said St. Paul Park has a local rack supply that accounts for a substantial portion of the volume, adding that open RIN exposure, when weighted across the two facilities, "will come down pretty dramatically."
SREs CVR already benefited from an SRE in August, when the EPA
granted Wynnewood a full exemption for its 2025 obligation. That cut the RFS liability on CVR's balance sheet by $253 million. The accrued RFS obligation stood at $230 million at Sept. 30, representing 113 million RINs marked to market at an average of $2.04.
Mandan has received a small refinery exemption (SRE) for 50% of its 2023, 2024 and 2025 obligations.
Even so, CFO Roberts said RIN costs "continue to disproportionately harm small and merchant refineries like ours." He said RINs now represent about 28 cents of every gallon at the pump.
-- Janet Mcgurty

REFINERY NEWS: Marathon Petroleum hits Q3 record utilization, lower Q4 expected
Refinery: 14 US refineries
Owner: Marathon Petroleum
Overall capacity: 2.986 million b/d
Units affected: N/A
Duration: Q4 2026
Marathon Petroleum ran its refineries at 100% utilization in the third quarter while carrying out planned turnaround work, CEO Maryann Mannen said on Nov. 3.
"Our third quarter results demonstrate the earnings power of MPC's value chains," she said on Marathon's Q3 results call. "Consistent with our commitment to safe and reliable operations, we achieved record third quarter utilization of 100% to meet market demand while executing our planned turnaround and maintenance activity."
Third-quarter total crude capacity utilization was 100% with 3.2 million b/d of crude throughput, with an R&M margin of $45.70/b compared with the $17.60/b in Q3 2025.
Marathon's USGC refineries ran at 99% utilization despite planned conversion unit downtime and delivered nearly $33/b.
However, margin capture fell to 93%, below the 112% capture rate recorded in Q2 2026.
"Secondary products were a material headwind, the largest we have seen on an absolute basis to capture as benchmark clean product prices increased more than secondary prices during the quarter," Marathon's CFO Maria Khoury said on the call.
She said weaker jet margins and seasonal inventory builds added pressure, which were partially offset by clean product margins and wider diesel-to-gasoline spreads.
In Q4, Marathon expects to process 2.8 million b/d of crude, representing a utilization rate of 95% with turnaround expenses of $230 million, mainly focused on conversion units on the US Gulf and West coasts, which is expected to "create a headwind to capture" as the work will limit Marathon's ability to upgrade certain products, she added.

Advantaged crude supply During Q4, Marathon expects to set records on heavy
Canadian crude processed, said Rick Hessling, Marathon's chief commercial officer.
Hessling noted that earlier Western Canada Select was holding a $32/b discount to WTI.
"That differential continues to widen out... And I think it may widen out further," he added, noting that high freight rates are challenging crude oil export economics, particularly for heavy and medium sour crudes. "Thankfully.... we have a small exposure to freight on the crude side as about 90% of our crude sourcing is from the US and Canada."
"And when you couple that with the Venezuelan and SPR barrels that have hit the US Gulf Coast, the diluents are being blended into heavy Canadian right now," he added, noting that they saw increased apportionments for both light and heavy crudes on Enbridge.
As of Nov. 2, WCS ex-Hardisty was holding a $32.84/b discount to WTI, while Venezuelan Merey was at a $30.93/b discount to WTI, according to Platts assessments. Platts is part of S&P Global Energy.
On the US West Coast, where Marathon has a large Los Angeles, California, plant as well as a smaller refinery in Anacortes, Washington, Hessling said Marathon has more than doubled the amount of California grades it has been running in its Los Angeles plant.
"When you think of the Pacific Northwest, we are actually running a lot of inland crudes at Anacortes, specifically to give us a competitive advantage over more expensive crudes, such as ANS," he said, referring to Alaska North Slope, which historically has been the benchmark crude for the region.
On the product side, he noted the difficulty of Asian barrels coming over to the USWC, adding that with high freight, "and you continue to have supply disruptions as well, causing them to not run at full rates."
"So we continue to see that as a nice tailwind for our West Coast assets," he added.
Streamlining of Midwest assets In the Midcontinent, Marathon sees a significant opportunity
with the expansion of the Laurel Pipe Line's bi-directional flows. "We are going to be a significant shipper.... here in November
and we will ramp up through the winter months when PADD 2 is predominantly long," he said.
"We see significant advantages specifically when you look at our Canton and Catlettsburg refinery," he added, referring to its Canton, Ohio, and Catlettsburg, Kentucky, refineries.
Also in the Midwest, Marathon said on Nov. 3 it would sell two of its Midcontinent refineries - St. Paul Park, Minnesota, and Mandan, North Dakota, to CVR Energy for $4.2 billion and use the proceeds to continue enhancing operations at its other refineries, primarily its two large US Gulf Coast refineries in Galveston Bay, Texas, and Garyville, Louisiana, which are currently the second and fourth largest US refineries. The transaction is expected to close at the end of 2026.
-- Janet Mcgurty

REFINERY NEWS: India's east coast refineries run normal post Murasu
Refinery: Paradip, Odisha, east coast of India
Owner: Indian Oil Corp.
Overall capacity: 300,000 b/d
Refinery: Vizag, Andhra Pradesh, India
Owner: Hindustan Petroleum Corporation, Ltd.
Overall capacity: 300,000 b/d
Indian refineries on the east coast, including Vizag and Paradip, have been running normal after cyclone Murasu made landfall, refinery executives said Nov. 3.
The cyclonic storm over the Bay of Bengal made landfall on the east coast on the morning of Nov. 3, they said.
Vizag refinery is located on the shores of the Bay of Bengal, while Paradip is located within five kilometers of the east coast.
The cyclonic storm weakened after it had made landfall near Ongole, in the Prakasam district of Andhra Pradesh, weather officials said.
The cyclone Murasu's speed ranged from 110 to 120 kilometers per hour and caused heavy rain across the east coast after landfall, the weather officials said.
Murasu is the second cyclone after cyclone Arnab, with a speed of 150 kmph, that hit the east coast Oct. 28, they added.
In September, Vizag processed 268,000 barrels/day, down 15% year over year, the oil ministry update said.
In the April-September period, or H1, Vizag processed 303,000 b/d, up 1.3% year over year, it said.
Vizag processed 320,000 b/d in 2025-26 (April-March) or FY26, up 4.6% year over year, it said.
Pradip processed 292,000 b/d in September, down 7.6% year over year, the ministry said.
In H1, Paradip processed 291,000 b/d, down 12%, it said. In FY26, Paradip processed 326,000 b/d, up 10.9% year over year, it said.
-- Ratnajyoti Dutta
REFINERY NEWS: CVR buying Marathon's North Dakota, Minnesota refineries
Refinery: St. Paul Park, Minnesota and Mandan, North Dakota
Owner: Marathon Petroleum
Overall capacity: 105,000 b/d and 72,000 b/d
Units affected: Plants
Duration: Deal to close end 2026
CVR Energy will pay Marathon Petroleum $4.2 billion for its St. Paul Park, Minnesota, and Mandan, North Dakota, refineries plus related marketing operations, doubling the number of refineries it owns and increasing refining capacity by over 85%.
"We are thrilled to deliver on our plan to strategically grow and diversify our business. We are acquiring two well-maintained,

highly productive refineries from Marathon that have generated more than $1.1 billion of EBITDA for the first nine months of 2026," said Dane Neumann, CVR's CEO, in a Nov. 3 statement.
The $4.2 billion transaction is expected to close by the end of 2026, subject to customary closing conditions and regulatory clearance, including US antitrust review.
Trafigura will invest $800 million in CVR equity and become the acquired refineries' exclusive crude supplier and intermediation counterparty, with certain product offtake arrangements, according to a separate statement from Trafigura.
"We intend to fund the transaction with a mix of common equity, cash and debt, and we plan to redeem our outstanding 2028 senior notes. The equity portion is intended to be funded by a large common equity investment from Trafigura, along with a $500 million equity commitment from IEP and approximately $650 million of cash from the CVI balance sheet," said Neumann on the CVR's third quarter results call held Nov. 3. . IEP is Icahn Enterprises, which holds about a 71% stake in CVR.
" We have $2.5 billion of committed bridge financing from our banks, which we expect to replace with syndicated secured debt financing prior to closing, and we expect to draw $300 million on an upsized $1.1 billion ABL facility," he added.
The transaction would significantly expand CVR's refining business, which centers on its 132,000 b/d Coffeyville, Kansas, and 74,500 b/d Wynnewood, Oklahoma, refineries, increasing its crude refining capacity from the current 206,500 b/d to 383,500 b/d.
The St. Paul Park refinery is located near the Twin Cities market and processes crude oil into gasoline, distillates and other refined products. Its location gives it access to crude supplies moving into the Midwest as well as a major regional refined-products market.
The Mandan refinery serves markets in North Dakota and the surrounding region. Located near the Bakken producing area, the plant has access to regional crude supply and produces transportation fuels for markets across the Northern Plains.
Acquiring the two plants would broaden CVR's geographic exposure beyond its existing Midcontinent refining operations and increase its presence in markets tied to both Canadian and domestic crude production.
For Marathon, the sale would reduce the size of its refining portfolio while allowing the company to monetize two relatively smaller plants compared with several of its large-scale refining complexes elsewhere in the US.
The marketing assets included in the transaction would also give CVR downstream outlets for fuel produced by the acquired refineries, adding a commercial component to what otherwise would be a significant expansion of its refining capacity.
CVR's existing refining system includes the Coffeyville and Wynnewood refineries. Adding St. Paul Park and Mandan would give the company a four-refinery system spread across Minnesota, North Dakota, Kansas and Oklahoma, increasing its operational and geographic diversification.
The transaction comes as US refiners continue to weigh the
